--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I'll analyze the mission carefully before designing machines.

**Mission Analysis:**
- Score increases by 1 per robot OUTSIDE the target region (BLACK floor strip with BLUE walls)
- Target: get all robots to the BLACK-floored strip bordered by BLUE walls
- The other BLACK strip has GREEN walls; the middle is GRAY

**Key perceptions available:**
- Floor color: BLACK (both strips) or GRAY (middle)
- Wall color: BLUE (target) or GREEN (wrong strip) - detectable via camera
- Neighbors within 0.5m

**Strategy:**
1. Robots should move toward BLUE walls (GoToColor with blue=3)
2. Once on BLACK floor near blue walls, stay there
3. Need to distinguish target BLACK from wrong BLACK: can see BLUE walls from target strip

**State design:**
- State: Explore/GoToBlue walls → if on BLACK floor and see BLUE, stop or stay
- GoToColor blue (clr=3) should attract toward blue walls
- GoAwayColor green (clr=2) to avoid green-walled strip
- Floor=BLACK with probability to stop if near blue walls

Let me design 14 machines with varying strategies:

**Machine 1 (elitism base - strong blue-seeking):** 2 states: GoToColor(blue), Stop. Transition on black floor.

**Machine 2:** 3 states exploring different combinations.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I need to see the results from Round 2 to know which machines performed best. Since no scores were provided, I'll reason from first principles and diversify my approach.

**Re-reading the mission:** "AGGREGATION mission" - wait, the scoring rule says score increases by 1 for every robot OUTSIDE the target region (BLACK floor with BLUE walls). So this is actually about getting robots INTO the target strip, not aggregating them together. The key challenge: both strips have BLACK floors, so floor color alone can't distinguish them. Robots must use BLUE wall perception to find the right strip.

**Key insight:** GoToColor with clr=3 (blue) should drive robots toward the blue-bordered strip. GoAwayColor with clr=2 (green) should push robots away from the wrong strip. On BLACK floor, if robot sees BLUE walls → it's in the right place → Stop.

**Refined strategy:**
- Primary: GoToColor blue (clr=3) to approach target
- Secondary: Stop when on BLACK floor AND seeing blue walls
- Avoid: GoAwayColor green (clr=2) to escape wrong strip
- Exploration behavior to find blue walls when none visible

I'll keep the best topologies from round 1 and add variations:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I notice I haven't been given any score feedback between rounds. I need to reason more carefully about what might be working.

**Critical re-analysis:**

The mission is called "AGGREGATION" but the scoring is about robots being in the target BLACK strip (blue walls). Wait - let me re-read: "score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region." So it's a coverage/placement problem, not traditional aggregation.

**What robots can perceive:**
- Floor: BLACK (in either strip) or GRAY (middle)
- Camera: BLUE walls (target strip) or GREEN walls (wrong strip)
- The color IDs: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan

**Best strategy logic:**
1. If robot sees BLUE walls → it's near the target → GoToColor blue or stay
2. If robot is on BLACK floor AND sees blue → it's IN the target → Stop
3. If robot sees GREEN walls → move away (GoAwayColor green=2... wait, green=2? Let me check: clr options are {1,2,3,4,5,6}. The wall colors are BLUE and GREEN. Blue=3, Green=2 seems right)
4. If on GRAY floor → explore/move toward blue walls

**Key concern:** My FSMs may be over-complicated. The simplest effective machine might just be GoToColor(blue) with a Stop transition on BLACK floor + sees blue. Let me also try pure GoToColor(blue) with no stopping - maybe stopping on any black floor is wrong since the other strip also has black floor.

**Important fix:** Stop should only trigger when seeing BLUE (to confirm it's the right black strip), not just any BLACK floor.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still haven't received score feedback, which means I need to reason more carefully about what's likely working and what isn't.

**Deep re-analysis:**

The mission name is "AGGREGATION" but the scoring is about robots being in the target strip. Let me reconsider - maybe I'm misreading the task. The score = robots outside target region. Target = BLACK floor with BLUE walls.

**Critical insight I may have missed:** The camera detects colored objects (robots' LEDs and walls). Wall colors BLUE and GREEN are fixed features. But do robots actually perceive wall colors with the camera? The camera detects objects by their LED color - so walls may not be detectable this way unless they emit light.

**Re-reading condition C=7:** "the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera" - this likely detects robot LEDs, not wall colors! If so, my entire strategy of GoToColor(blue) and detecting blue walls via camera is wrong.

**What actually works:**
- Floor color: BLACK (both strips) and GRAY (middle) - reliably detectable
- GoToColor may navigate toward colored blobs - walls may or may not be detected
- The safest approach: navigate using floor color alone, plus exploration

**Revised strategy:**
- On GRAY floor: explore/move (you're in the middle, need to reach a strip)
- On BLACK floor: you might be in either strip. Need to stay if it's the right one.
- Problem: can't distinguish strips without wall detection

Since I can't know if wall detection works, I should hedge: include machines that use floor-only logic AND machines that use camera-based wall detection.

**Floor-only approach:** Move randomly until BLACK floor, then stop. Half the time robots land in the right strip = ~50% score reduction. Not great but guaranteed.

**Better floor-only:** Explore on GRAY, stop on BLACK. If wrong strip, small probability to restart exploration.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still have no score feedback, which is unusual. Let me completely rethink from scratch with fresh eyes.

**Fundamental rethink:**

The mission says "AGGREGATION" but scoring is robots outside target strip. Let me reconsider what behaviors actually make sense physically:

1. **GoToColor(blue, clr=3)**: Moves toward blue-colored objects. If arena walls bordering the target strip are BLUE, this should work - the robot moves toward the blue wall until it's in that strip. This seems like the most direct solution.

2. **Floor detection**: BLACK in both strips, GRAY in middle. Can't distinguish target from non-target using floor alone.

3. **The stopping condition**: Once in the target strip, the robot should STOP. But how does it know it's in the RIGHT black strip? It should still see BLUE walls nearby.

**Most likely issue with my designs:** I've been using `--clr` values but I'm not sure if 3=blue. Let me re-examine: "clr one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}" and wall colors are BLUE and GREEN. Standard robot color encoding in ARGoS often uses: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan. So blue=3, green=2 seems correct.

**Alternative hypothesis:** Maybe the simplest 1-state GoToColor(blue) machine works best - just always move toward blue walls, forever. No stopping needed because moving toward blue walls keeps you near them.

**Another thought:** Maybe I should try GoToColor with different color IDs in case my mapping is wrong.

Let me try a wider variety including single-state machines and different color IDs:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I have received no score feedback across 6 rounds. This is very concerning - I'm essentially designing blind. Let me radically rethink.

**Complete strategic reset:**

Since I've been getting no feedback, I must assume my designs are either all similarly mediocre or I'm making a systematic error. Let me think about what the AGGREGATION mission truly requires.

**Key insight I may have missed:** This is called "AGGREGATION" - perhaps the actual goal IS aggregation (robots clustering together), and the "target region" framing means: robots need to be in the same strip as each other. The blue-walled strip is the designated meeting point.

**What if walls aren't perceivable?** In standard ARGoS e-puck setups, the camera detects other robots' LEDs, not wall colors. If walls aren't detectable via camera (C=7), then all my GoToColor(blue) and camera-based detection is useless.

**If only floor color works:**
- GRAY = middle, BLACK = either strip
- Strategy: move until BLACK floor, then stop with high probability
- But ~50% land in wrong strip

**If we can use robot LEDs as signals:**
- Robots in target strip light up BLUE (cle=3... wait, cle options are {0,4,5,6}: 0=off, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)
- No blue LED available! So robots can't signal "I'm in target" with blue LEDs

**Realistic approach - floor color only:**
- Explore on GRAY/non-black
- Stop on BLACK floor
- Some escape probability to avoid getting stuck in wrong strip

Let me also try FlockCenter and Attraction behaviors - maybe aggregating robots helps them collectively navigate.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I have now gone 7 rounds with zero score feedback. This strongly suggests either all my machines are performing similarly (all bad or all similar), or there's a systematic issue with my approach.

**Radical rethink - what am I missing?**

Re-reading the problem: "The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE."

The robots start in the CENTER (gray area). They need to reach one specific black strip.

**Critical question: Can GoToColor actually navigate toward walls?**
In ARGoS, GoToColor uses the camera which detects colored objects. Walls can indeed be colored and detectable. So GoToColor(blue=3) should work to navigate toward blue walls.

**But wait** - if robots start in the center and the blue walls are at one end, GoToColor(blue) should drive them directly there. This is a very simple, direct solution. Why might it fail?
- If robots reach the wall and get stuck against it (they'd be in the strip - good!)
- GoToColor makes robots move toward blue = they enter the blue-walled strip = score goes down

**Maybe the issue is I keep adding Stop states.** If a robot stops anywhere (including on gray floor or wrong strip), that's bad. Maybe the best machine is simply: **always GoToColor(blue), never stop.**

Let me try the most aggressive simple approaches and also completely different strategies:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
Still no score feedback after 8 rounds. I need to think about this differently.

**What I know for certain:**
- The mission is AGGREGATION (robots must be in the blue-walled black strip)
- Robots start in the gray center
- I've been trying GoToColor(blue), floor-based stopping, etc.
- No feedback means I can't tell what's working

**Most likely reason for no feedback:** The system may only report scores when rounds complete with notable results, OR I'm misunderstanding the format. Either way, I must design the best possible machines from first principles.

**Let me think about what ACTUALLY moves robots to the target:**

1. `GoToColor(blue=3)` - directly navigates toward blue walls. This is the most semantically correct behavior. A robot doing this forever will press against the blue wall = inside target strip = score 0 for that robot.

2. The problem: robots starting in gray center need to FIND the blue walls. GoToColor will work if blue walls are visible from the center. If not, they need to explore first.

3. **Key realization:** `--n0 0` means zero transitions = robot stays in this state forever. A single-state GoToColor(blue) machine that never stops is actually very strong - it keeps driving toward blue indefinitely.

4. **Second realization:** Maybe my color mapping is wrong. Let me try ALL colors systematically in single-state GoToColor machines to find which one corresponds to blue walls.

**Final analysis:**

The key insight I keep returning to: GoToColor(blue=3) should directly navigate robots to blue walls = target strip. A single-state machine doing this forever is conceptually perfect. But I've been unable to verify if it works.

Let me think about what could go wrong with GoToColor(blue):
1. If blue walls aren't visible from center → robots wander randomly (GoToColor defaults to random walk when target not visible)
2. Color ID mapping might be wrong
3. The behavior might be working fine and I just have no feedback

**For the final round, I'll:**
- Cover ALL 6 color IDs with single-state GoToColor (diagnostic sweep)
- Cover ALL 6 color IDs with single-state GoAwayColor (maybe avoiding green=wrong strip works)
- Include best composite machines from previous rounds
- Try GoAwayColor for each color

This final round should be comprehensive to identify the correct color mapping and best strategy simultaneously.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
